This note reports the first search for leptoquark signals in the 𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state with the ATLAS
experiment, motivated by the 𝑅(𝐷
(∗)) anomaly, using the Run-2 dataset with 140 fb−1 of an
integrated luminosity at √
𝑠 = 13 TeV.

188 1 Executive Summary
189 1.1 Target
This is a search for leptoquark signals in the 𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state, motivated by the 𝑅(𝐷
(∗) 190 ) anomalies by the
191 BaBar, Belle and LHCb experiments. It is a completely new phase space, previously not explored by
ATLAS and CMS1
192 . Since the signal strength scales with the 4th power of the coupling constant, the
193 sensitivity gain from increased integrated luminosity is rather limited (of course the limit on the signal
194 cross-section will be improved by adding more data, though). We agreed with PC/PubCom and relevant
195 conveners to use the Run-2 dataset only and aim for an early publication by the end of 2025.
196 1.2 Context and Motivation
197 Motivate this analysis in 1 paragraph: why is this signature interesting? Which kind of
198 models are you probing?
While the 𝑅(𝐷
(∗) 199 ) anomaly requires both 𝑏𝜏 and 𝑐𝜈 couplings of the leptoquark, previous ATLAS
200 and CMS searches, focusing on the 𝜏𝜏 final state, were sensitive only to the 𝑏𝜏 coupling and typically
201 fixed the 𝑐𝜈 coupling to a small value. This assumption was motivated not by experimental constraints
202 but by theoretical considerations: a general UV completion to suppress Δ𝐹 = 2 transitions. A more
203 model-independent approach is crucial, as the UV model may include mechanisms that naturally suppress
204 flavor transitions, such as the GIM mechanism in the SM. Our search in the 𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state is sensitive to
205 both 𝑏𝜏 and 𝑐𝜈 couplings simultaneously, and thus uniquely probes scenarios with larger 𝑐𝜈 couplings that
206 were previously unexplored.
207 How is the analysis done in 1 paragraph: what are the main BG processes and how do you
208 estimate them (are they MC- or data-driven, what is the general idea of the control regions,
209 . . . ), general characteristics of the PL fit (which distribution, binned?, . . . )
210 We will perform a simple cut-and-count analysis with two single-bin SRs. Events with high-𝑝T hadronic 𝜏
and high-𝐸
miss
T
211 are selected. Two SRs are categorized by the 𝑏-jet 𝑝T: one with a high-𝑝T 𝑏-jet for an
212 on-shell leptoquark signal decaying to 𝑏𝜏; and the other with low-𝑝T 𝑏-jet for a non-resonant 𝜏𝜈 signal via
213 𝑡-channel exchange of the leptoquark. The main SM background is (𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈)+jets. Top-quark production
214 (𝑡𝑡¯ and single-𝑡) is subdominant. For these components, dedicated CRs are prepared and involved in
1 There was a 𝑊+𝑏 measurements in the past, but it employed 𝑒 and 𝜇 channels only [1]. There were searches for SUSY or
Leptoquark signals in 𝑏, 𝜏 and 𝐸
miss
T
final states, but they required multiple 𝑏 or 𝜏 [2–4], but we couldn’t find searches in
ATLAS with a single 𝜏, single 𝑏-jet and large 𝐸
miss
T
.
9

=== PDF PAGE 10 ===
ATLAS DRAFT
215 the likelihood to give a constraint from data to the MC simulation. WCR is defined by replacing the
216 𝜏 in the SR with an electron or muon. TopCR requires an additional electron or muon to the SR. The
217 background estimation strategy is checked carefully in the VRs. The SM background with fake 𝜏 lepton
218 (mostly from (𝑍 → 𝜈𝜈)+jets) is expected to have very small yields, and will be estimated by the MC
219 simulation directly, after a careful check in (𝑍 → ℓℓ)+fake-𝜏 VR. For us, the fake 𝜏 rejection is not a major
220 concern, and the sensitivity depends mostly on the signal efficiency. We will therefore use the RNNTau
221 identification, without waiting for the latest recommendation for the GNTau, for which we already have a
222 CP recommendation for the Run-2 dataset.
223 1.3 Milestones
224 The analysis team is very small, with 1 PhD student and 3 staff members. The following table summarizes
225 the tasks to be worked on by analysis team. This is not a complete analysis outline but only an overview of
226 the further steps to be taken as of the time of writing. Details are not provided here but in the dedicated
227 sections throughout this note.
228 Glance entry: https://atlas-glance.cern.ch/atlas/analysis/analyses/details.php?ref_
229 code=ANA-EXOT-2025-06

231 2 Introduction
232 A test of the lepton flavor universality (LFU) has been made via the ratio of the branching fractions
𝑅(𝐷
(∗)) = B (𝐵 → 𝐷
(∗)𝜏𝜈)/B (𝐵 → 𝐷
(∗) 233 ℓ𝜈) by the BaBar[5, 6], Belle[7–10], Belle-II[11, 12] and
234 LHCb[13–16] experiments, where ℓ = 𝑒, 𝜇 (ℓ = 𝜇 for LHCb). The latest average of these measurements
calculated by the HFLAV collaboration[17] is shown in Figure 2.1. It reports 𝑅(𝐷) and 𝑅(𝐷
∗
235 ) exceed the
Standard Model (SM) predictions[18–27] by 2.5𝜎 and 2.3𝜎 respectively. Considering the 𝑅(𝐷)-𝑅(𝐷
∗
236 )
correlation of −0.374, the resulting combined 𝜒
2
237 is 17.63 for 2 degree of freedom, corresponding to a
𝑝-value of 1.48 × 10−4
238 . The difference with the SM predictions corresponds to about 3.8𝜎. This can be a
239 hint of a new physics beyond the SM, and it is important for Belle-II and LHCb to follow it up with more
240 data.

Figure 2.1: Average of 𝑅(𝐷
∗
) and 𝑅(𝐷)[17] measured by the BaBar[5, 6], Belle[7–10], Belle-II[11, 12], and
LHCb[14–16] experiments.
A possible solution to explain the 𝑅(𝐷
(∗) 241 ) anomaly is to introduce a hypothetical new particle, a leptoquark,
242 which couples to both leptons and quarks, as shown in Fig. 2.2. A comprehensive program to search for
243 leptoquark signals with 𝜏 leptons in the final state has been conducted by the ATLAS and CMS experiments,
244 for both scalar and vector leptoquark scenarios [28–31]. Assuming the leptoquark pair production, the cross
245 section depends solely on the strong coupling constant at the leading order, making the searches mostly
246 model-independent. Signals with leptoquark masses below approximately 1.5–1.8 TeV were excluded by
247 such searches. For the single leptoquark production, searches in the 𝑏𝜏𝜏 final state were performed. In the
248 case of the large coupling constant, 𝑡-channel exchange of the leptoquark is expected to lead to an excess of
249 events in the high-mass region of SM processes. ATLAS and CMS performed such a “non-resonant search”
for the leptoquark in the 𝜏𝜏 final state. ATLAS excluded the region approximately 𝜆𝑏𝜏/𝑀LQ > 1 TeV−1
250 ,
251 where 𝜆𝑏𝜏 and 𝑀LQ are the coupling constant and mass of the leptoquark, defined in Section 3. For the
vector leptoquark models, the result partially tested a parameter region consistent with 𝑅(𝐷
(∗) 252 ) anomaly.

253 CMS had a similar sensitivity, but observed a small excess of events at the high 𝑚𝜏 𝜏, leading to a weaker
254 observed limit on 𝜆𝑏𝜏/𝑀.
255 All of the results above are summarized in Figures 2.4 and 2.5 for ATLAS and CMS, respectively. For
256 these analyses, signal strength was assumed to depend only on 𝑏𝜏 coupling of the leptoquark, but the
𝑅(𝐷
(∗) 257 ) anomaly requires both 𝑏𝜏 and 𝑐𝜈 couplings.
258 This note reports the first search for the leptoquark signals in the 𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state with the ATLAS detector,
259 which is sensitive to the product of both couplings. As shown in Figure 2.3, our target signal is exactly
the same structure as the 𝑅(𝐷
(∗) 260 ) diagram (Fig. 2.2 (b)). The presence of a 𝑏-jet mitigates the effect of
261 interference with the SM processes, and suppress the background in SRs strongly. This phase space has
262 never been explored by ATLAS/CMS. CMS had a leptoquark interpretation in the 𝜏𝜈 final state in their
263 𝑊′ → 𝜏𝜈 paper[32], but the sensitivity was not so high since it did not require any 𝑏-jets.
264 For the strong physics motivation described in Section 3, it has been justified to use the Run-2 dataset only
of an integrated luminosity 140 fb−1
265 , and we aim to publish the results by the end of 2025. We agreed with
266 PC/PubComm/EXOT and LPX conveners about the publication strategy. Some more detailed discussion
267 about the physics motivation is found in Section 3.

332 4 Monte Carlo Simulation
333 In this section, some details of the Monte-Carlo simulation used in this analysis are discussed.
334 4.1 Signal Samples

336 LQ model is based on Ref. [37], and the original 𝑈1 vector LQ model files are provided in Ref. [38], but
337 the same CKM matrix between the LQ Lagrangian and the SM is introduced. The detailed configuration
338 of the Universal FeynRules Output (UFO) model files is found in Ref. [39]. The full Lagrangian related to
339 the vector LQ used in the UFO model [37] is defined as:

345 The parameter 𝜅 introduces the LQ couplings to the SM gauge-boson fields. The model with 𝜅 = 1 is the
346 minimal 𝑈1 model, and the one with 𝜅 = 0 is called as the Yang-Mills 𝑈1 model.
347 Our MC sample is 𝜏𝜈𝜏 with up-to 2 jets, where “jet”s here are gluon, 𝑢, 𝑑, 𝑐, 𝑠, or 𝑏 quark. We use MC20a/d/e
configurations at √
348 𝑠 = 13 TeV. Diagrams with 2 new physics couplings (NP == 2) are categorized as
“BSM”, while those with NP2
349 == 2 are categorized as “interference” with the SM 𝜏𝜈 processes. The
350 MadGraph can identify BSM and interference event-by-event. For the initial test (Section A.1), BSM,
interference, and BSM+internfernce without SM (NP2
351 > 0) events are generated separately at each mass.
352 They are called bsm, inf, and bi, respectively. These words can be found in the dataset name of our
353 signal samples. Since the 𝑈1 leptoquark is exchanged in both 𝑠-channel (resonance) and 𝑡-channel
354 (non-resonant) topologies, and the full matrix element (ME) is simulated coherently, these components
355 cannot be disentangled in the generation step. Signal samples include not only the target diagrams of
356 this analysis in Fig. 2.3, but also for example a pair production of LQs decaying into 𝑈1𝑈1 → 𝑏𝜏𝑐𝜈,
357 depending on the LQ mass. The NNPDF3.0NLO (LHAPDF ID= 260800) and the five-flavor scheme are

386 5 Analysis Framework and Object Definitions
387 Object definitions and event pre-selections are summarized in this section.
388 5.1 Tau+X Framework
389 The analysis is based on the Tau+X framework developed under the Exotics Tau+X umbrella efforts.
390 The DAOD_PHYS derivation is used with p6479 (data) and p6490 (MC). Events are first preprocessed
391 by TopCPTookKit 2.17.1 for the calibrations by CP tools, selections of physics objects and event
392 pre-selections. AnalysisBase release is 25.2.51. The latest R22 recommended electron efficiency SF
393 setup is done manually through the official map file with the local checkout (ElectronEfficiencyCorrec￾394 tion/2015_2025/rel22.2/2025_Run2Rel22_Recommendation_v3/map0.txt). The modification can be found
395 on GitLab.
396 The selected events by TopCPToolKit are further processed by the FastFrame framework. At this stage,
397 some more detailed event selections are applied for SR/CR/VR and histograms/light-weight trees are saved,
398 that can be used as an input for the TRexFitter statistical framework directly.
399 5.2 Electron Selections
The “baseline” electron selections to be used for the overlap removal and 𝐸
miss
T
400 reconstruction are
401 summarized in Table 5.1. For the “signal” electrons used in the SR/CR/VR, we require HighPtCaloOnly
402 isolation on top of that, to suppress the high-𝑝T fake electrons.

416 5.4 Hadronic Tau Selections
The “baseline” tau selections to be used for the overlap removal and 𝐸
miss
T
417 reconstruction are summarized
418 in Table 5.3. We use the RNN-based tau identification, for which we already have a full set of CP

420 on top of the baseline tau (the LooseRNN identification with loose electron veto). The kinematics of taus
421 are also tighten from 20 GeV to 200 GeV. We do not have eVeto SF for TightRNN identification, but
422 agreed with TauCP group to use the eVeto SF for MediumRNN working point. In our SR, electron-faking-tau
423 contribution is very small, so no impact on the final result is expected.
424 The selection criteria are all implemented in the TauSelectionTool as part of the TauAnalysisTools.
425 Documentation can be found in the README-TauSelectionTool.rst.

473 6 Signal Region Optimization
474 In this section, the optimization of the signal region (SR) is discussed. Two SRs are optimized: one for the
475 resonance signal, and the other for the non-resonance signal. The optimal event selection is determined by
476 scanning the thresholds for particular kinematic variables, considering the correlation between them, to
477 maximize the significance 𝑍 [40]:
𝑍 =

478 where 𝑠 and 𝑏 represent the mean expected signal and background yields, respectively, 𝑛 = 𝑠 + 𝑏 and 𝜎 is
479 the assumed systematic uncertainty on the background expectation. We assumed 𝜎 = 30% in this study.

493 Resonance and Non-Resonance SRs are categorized by the 𝑏-jet 𝑝T. For the Resonance SR, 𝑝T > 250 GeV
494 is required. For the Non-Resonance SR, on the other hand, 50 < 𝑝T < 250 GeV is applied.
495 The scanned variables and their ranges are found in Table 6.1. We only tried two values, 0 and 800 GeV,

499 1. The background yields 𝑏 > 2;
500 2. The yield of a specific SM background process (e.g., 𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈) is set to 0 if it is a negative value.
501 The first constraint is set to ensure that we won’t have the zero-background estimation, even though this
502 would sacrifice the sensitivity. Also, it ensures that we have a proper minor background estimation for
503 the events with the negative weights under tight selection. As a result, the upper bound on the MC stat
504 uncertainty is set. Specifically, it’s suppressed to 31% for resonance SR and 40% for non-resonance-SR.
505 The numbers in the following sections are updated with the latest signal samples using the corrected
506 weights described in Appendix A. Only BSM samples with statistical uncertainties are considered here.
507 The interference with SM is expected to be less than 5% of the BSM yields as studied in Appendix I.

546 7 Background Estimation Strategy
547 The main source of the SM background in this analysis is (𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈)+jets, and top-quark events (𝑡𝑡¯ and
548 𝑊𝑡) are subdominant. For these two components, free normalization factors are introduced, as described in
549 Section 10. Dedicated CRs are defined to determine the normalization factors and are involved in the fit.
550 The data/MC comparison in CRs is discussed in Section 8.
Events with fake 𝜏 lepton (mainly (𝑍 → 𝜈𝜈)+jets for large 𝐸
miss
T
551 ) are minor source of the SM background
552 for this analysis. They are estimated by the simulation directly. Modeling of 𝑗 → 𝜏 fake rate in the
553 simulation is validated in (𝑍 → ℓℓ)+fake-𝜏 VR in Appendix D. Multi-jet events with both fake 𝜏 and
fake 𝐸
miss
T
554 are found to be negligible after the QCD cleaning cuts in Appendix C. The other background
555 (diboson, including real 𝜏, for which the modeling is relatively reliable) is also estimated by the simulation,
556 with conservative uncertainties on the cross sections.
557 Concept of the overall background estimation strategy is shown in Figure 7.1.
WVR1tau
TopVR1tau*
Nb = 0
Nb = 1
(Nτ, Nlep) = (0, 1) (1, 1) (1, 0)
TopCR* SR

VR0tau
Nb = 2
* For TopCR and TopVR1tau, looser cuts for tau pT, mEt and mT are applied for statistics
Figure 7.1: Definitions of SR, CR and VR of this analysis.
558 7.1 WCR
559 WCR is defined by replacing the 𝜏 in the SR to the electron or muon, to take (𝑊 → ℓ𝜈)+jets in the same
560 topology (ℓ = 𝑒, 𝜇). To gain the statistics, and to suppress the top-quark contributions, 𝑏-jet veto is required

instead of 𝑁𝑏 = 1 in the SR. Single lepton triggers are used instead of the 𝐸

To define the WCRs, 𝑚

565 Non-Resonance WCRs and reconstruct 𝑚 𝑗ℓ . The lepton 𝑝T is required to be greater than 200 GeV. The
566 other selection cuts are completely the same as the SR, as summarized in Table 7.1.
567 For the Non-Resonance WCR, the purity of 𝑊+jets is about 90%. It is about 70% for the Resonance WCR,
568 and about 20% contribution from diboson events is expected.
569 Signal contamination is assessed using samples including both hadronic and leptonic decaying taus. The
coupling parameter choice is (𝑀LQ, 𝛽23

588 7.3 Validation Regions
589 Estimated normalization factors for 𝑊+jets and top-quark events are checked in the VRs. We discussed with
590 LPX conveners about the blinding strategy at the tau+X meeting on 16 May. If the signal contamination
591 is less than 10% level, assuming a signal of just about any size that has not been rejected in previous 𝜏𝜏
592 analysis, basically it is ok to see the data (although we need to check carefully e.g. the contamination of
593 𝑊′ → 𝜏𝜈 searches).
594 The signal contamination is checked by signal samples generated with both leptonic decay and hadronic
decay 𝜏. For the coupling parameters, we choose (𝑀LQ, 𝛽23
𝐿
595 ) = (1.5 TeV, 0.2) for the Resonance VRs and
(𝑀LQ, 𝛽23
𝐿
596 ) = (2.5 TeV, 1) for the Non-Resonance VRs, at 𝑔𝑈 = 1.5. These parameters are not excluded
597 by 𝜏𝜏 search.

598 7.3.1 VR0tau
599 For the first VR to check the 𝑏-vetoed WCR to 𝑏-tagged SR transition, the same selections as WCR (𝑁ℓ = 1
600 and 𝑁𝜏 = 0) but with 𝑁𝑏 = 1 are required, as summarized in Table 7.1. The background composition in
601 this VR0tau is found to be almost the same as SR.
602 We found the signal contamination is small, since we consider the small coupling of LQ to 1st and
603 2nd generation leptons, and the tau lepton decay hadronically contributes little in this VR. The signal
604 contamination by the leptonic decay of tau is (1.3 ± 0.1)% and (0.7 ± 0.1)% for the Resonance and
605 Non-resonance VR0tau.
606 7.3.2 WVR1tau and TopVR1tau
607 The other VRs to check the 𝑒/𝜇 CRs to 𝜏 SR transition are defined by the similar selections as SR but with
608 𝑁𝑏 = 0 (WVR1tau) and 𝑁𝑏 = 2 (TopVR1tau), as summarized in Table 7.3 and Table 7.4. The selection in
609 TopVR1tau is relaxed due to the low statistics in the high 𝑚T region.
610 The purity of 𝑊+jets is found to be (65.8 ± 3.4)% and (84 ± 11)% in Resonance and Non-Resonance
611 WVR1tau, respectively. The number of events from the simulation expectation is 32 events for resonance
612 WVR1tau, and 43 events for non-resonance WVR1tau. Meanwhile, (75.0 ± 4.6)% and (87.1 ± 5.1)%
613 of events in Resonance and Non-resonance TopVR1tau are 𝑡𝑡¯ and single-𝑡, respectively. The number of
614 expected events is 15 and 36 for Resonance and Non-resonance TopVR1tau.
615 In WVR1tau, the signal contamination is (1.7 ± 0.1)% and (12.6 ± 1.7)% for the Resonance and Non￾616 Resonance VRs, respectively. In TopVR1tau, the signal contamination is (1.2 ± 0.1)% and (0.6 ± 0.03)%
617 for the Resonance and Non-Resonance VRs, respectively.

618 8 Data/MC Comparisons
619 The ratio of observed data to MC simulation is checked in CRs and VRs for several kinematic variables.
620 Only statistical uncertainties are considered. We observed a small discrepancy (<∼ 1.5𝜎) for the data/MC
621 ratio between the electron and muon channels in the WCR, but concluded that it is coming from the
622 statistical fluctuation. Detailed studies are found in Appendix F. In the final fit, we will combine 𝑒 and 𝜇
623 channels to increase the statistics, and use the common normalization factors for them.
624 8.1 WCR
625 WCRs are defined in Section 7.1. The 𝑏-tagging requirement is flipped to the 𝑏-veto. The lepton and
626 leading jet are used instead of 𝜏 and 𝑏-jet in the SR.
627 8.1.1 Resonance WCR
628 The results of data/MC comparison in Resonance WCR are shown in Figure 8.1 and Figure 8.2 in the
629 electron and muon channels. The number of observed data is 243 events for the electron channel and 219
630 events for the muon channel. The ratios of the observed data events to the MC expectation are 1.25 ± 0.09
631 in the electron channel and 1.08 ± 0.08 in the muon channel. No distinct slope is found in each distribution.
632 The difference between e- and mu-channels is 1.4𝜎.
633 8.1.2 Non-Resonance WCR
634 The results of data/MC comparison in non-resonance WCR are shown in Figure 8.3 and Figure 8.4 in the
635 electron and muon channels. The number of observed data is 474 events for the electron channel and
636 398 events for the muon channel. The ratios of observed number of data events to those of simulation
637 are 1.00 ± 0.09 and 0.80 ± 0.08 in the electron and muon channels. The discrepancy between e- and mu￾638 channel is 1.7𝜎. No distinct slope is found in each distribution.

653 In general, the normalization factor for top-quark sample is lower than unity, around 0.8. The study of
654 systematic uncertainties of the top-quark simulated events is ongoing to check whether they can cover it.

658 to 1 for both regions. The results are shown in Figure 8.9. No large difference in shape is observed.

685 9 Systematic Uncertainties
686 9.1 Experimental sources
687 List of experimental sources of systematic uncertainty considered in this analysis is shown in next pages.
688 Symmetrization of (up-down)/2 or (abs(up)+abs(min))/2 is performed for most systematics due to the
689 limited statistics of the background in SR, except for JER, mET, and ttbar NNLO uncertainties.
690 9.1.1 Luminosity and pileup reweighting
691 The uncertainty in the combined 2015–2018 integrated luminosity is 0.83% [41], obtained using the
692 LUCID-2 detector [42] for the primary luminosity measurements, complemented by measurements using
693 the inner detector and calorimeters.
694 The uncertainty associated to the pileup reweighting is followed by prescription in Ref. [43]. The scale
695 factor is applied to the data sample, to adjust the 𝜇 value for better data/MC agreement on the number of
696 vertices distribution after the reweighting. The nominal factor is 1/1.03, with the variation from 1/1.07 to
697 1/0.99.
698 9.1.2 Tau energy scale and efficiency calibration uncertainties
699 The latest 2025 prerecommendation is used with AnalysisBase,25.2.51.
700 Tau energy scale (TES) The in-situ measurements using 𝑍 → 𝜏𝜏 events (INSITUEXP, FIT) covering
701 from low-𝑝T region were performed. Uncertainties at high-𝑝T (DETECTOR, PHYSICSLIST, etc.)
702 correspond to old uncertainties derived from test beam and MC comparison, which should still
703 be used too. The TES and related uncertainties are provided through the TauSmearingTool with
704 “2025-prerec” and “mc20” configurations. The correction is less than 1% with absolute uncertainty
705 of less than 1.5%.
706 Tau efficiency SFs Uncertainties on the scale factors for tau reconstruction, RNN-based identification and
707 eVeto are considered. TauEfficiencyCorrectionTool is configured with “2025-prerec” and “mc20”
708 options. Tau reconstruction efficiency uncertainty is TAUS_TRUEHADTAU_EFF_RECO_TOTAL.
709 Tau ID efficiencies were estimated for 1-prong and 3-prong taus separately. Uncorrelated NPs are
710 assigned for different 𝑝T bins. Central value of the SFs is around 1 in all 𝑝T bins, with 5–8%
711 (1-prong) and 6–11% (3-prong) uncertainties for the Tight ID working point.
60

=== PDF PAGE 61 ===
ATLAS DRAFT
9.1.3 Jet and 𝑬
miss
T
712 uncertainties
The R22 “Phase-1” pre-recommendation is used for JES/JER, pileup subtraction and 𝐸
miss
T
713 uncertainties.
714 CategoryReduction scheme (about 30+5 NPs) is used for JES uncertainties, while FullJER (13+2 NPs)
715 scheme for JER.
716 MC-based steps of the calibration were derived using R22 MC20 FullSimulation samples. Extra
717 uncertainties are added to take into account differences between R21 and R22 (updated topocluster noise
718 thresholds and tracking/vertexing changes):
719 • JESUnc_VertexingAlg_PreRec
720 • JESUnc_Noise_PreRec
721 For in-situ calibration, R21 studies are used with extra uncertainties to correct for non-closure when the
722 R21 in-situ calibrations are applied to R22 samples:
723 • EtaIntercalibration_NonClosure_PreRec
724 • InSitu_NonClosure_PreRec
725 • JERUnc_Noise_PreRec
726 Only for AF3 (signal) samples: extra uncertainty coming from jet response and resolution non-closure
727 between FullSim and AF3 is applied.
728 • RelativeNonClosure_AF3
729 • JERUnc_AF3vsFullSim_AF3_PreRec
730 We do NOT use the MC-to-MC calibration option for JES flavor response uncertainty. We do NOT follow
731 the JES precision reduced flavor uncertainties prescription, so the Flavor_Response and Composition
732 uncertainties are calculated with the default conservative option.
733 The JER uncertainties smear both (pseudo-)data and MC events [44]. Shifting a given NP by +1𝜎 will
734 correspond with smearing either the (pseudo-)data or MC, while shifting the same NP by −1𝜎 will
735 correspond with smearing the MC or (pseudo-)data. If the uncertainty for a given component crosses zero,
736 then this will change whether (pseudo)-data or MC is smeared, thus properly retaining the anti-correlated
737 behavior implied by the uncertainty, rather than enforcing a correlated behavior. Some more de￾738 tails in https://twiki.cern.ch/twiki/bin/view/AtlasProtected/JetUncertaintiesRel22#
739 Different_smearing_options.
740 9.1.4 Flavor tagging efficiency
741 Uncertainties on 𝑏-tagging efficiency SFs are evaluated by following the latest FTAG recommenda￾742 tion:https://ftag.docs.cern.ch/recommendations/. The Medium eigenvector reduction scheme
743 is currently used.
744 The GN2v01 tagger is used, and the recommendation is released in June 2025 (CDI file: 13TeV/MC20_2025-
745 06-17_GN2v01_v4.root).

754 9.2 Theoretical uncertainties
755 Since we will just use the single-bin SRs, theoretical uncertainties for the main background components,
756 𝑊+jets and top quark events, are estimated only on the “transfer factor” 𝑇 𝐹 from CR to SRs/VRs. Note
757 that we only consider the uncertainty of 𝑊+jets (Top) when propagating it from WCR (TopCR) to SR/VR
758 due to the high purity of the CRs. Specifically, the contamination from Top backgrounds is 1.6% and 0.4%
759 in Resonance and Non-resonance WCRs, respectively. And the contamination from 𝑊+jets backgrounds is
760 4.8% and 3.4% in Resonance and Non-resonance TopCRs, respectively. Even considering a conservative
761 50% uncertainty, the contributions from 𝑊+jets and Top backgrounds in TopCRs and WCRs are marginal.
762 A simplified explanation of the concept here

781 9.2.1 𝑾 → 𝝉𝝂+jets
782 Following the prescription in PMG twiki page. Sherpa 2.2.11 𝑊+jets sample has the internal weights to
783 vary the following parameters.
784 QCD scale variations 7-point QCD scale variations stored as internal weights in the baseline samples,
785 from which the envelope around the nominal can be worked out. The scale uncertainty is then given
786 by the maximum shift of the envelope with respect to the nominal with the version of ME+PS, which
787 is the coherent variation of the matrix element and parton shower renormalization and factorization
788 QCD scales. This is found to have more shift to the nominal than another scale variation of ME only.
789 PDF The Hessian NNPDF3.0 NNLO uncertainty scheme is to be adopted, which can be accessed via the
790 LHAPDF tool as it has built-in methods to work out the combined PDF uncertainty. The central
791 PDF values for the NNPDF3.0nnlo (PDF261000), MMHT2014nnlo68cl (PDF25300) and CT14nnlo
792 (PDF13000) PDF sets are included as variations as well as two NNPDF3.0nnlo 𝛼𝑆 variations
793 (PDF269000 and PDF270000).
794 Electroweak Virtual Corrections Approximate NLO EW corrections are included as on-the-fly weights
795 using the electroweak virtual approximation. The electroweak and QCD components can be combined
796 using an additive, multiplicative, or exponentiated prescription.
797 The 10–30% difference (or 30–40% for some generators) of the ATLAS data from SM expectations are
798 reported for 𝑊/𝑍 plus 𝑏-jets cross sections [1, 45–47]. Since we use the WCRs with 𝑏-veto and extrapolate
799 the normalization to SRs using the heavy flavor fraction in the MC sample, an additional conservative 20%
800 uncertainty is assigned for the resonance and non-resonance regions with 𝑏-jets, respectively. A detailed
801 study is addressed in the Appendix J. The sensitivity to the signal search is decreased by 2.3% with this
802 uncertainty. The discrepancy of the observed data from the SM expectation, discussed in Section 10 and
803 Appendix G is relaxed from 2.8 to 2.3𝜎 with this additional uncertainty and other systematics included.
804 9.2.2 Top quarks
805 Following the prescription in PMG twiki page.
806 Matrix element (NLO matching) Difference from the alternative sample with Powheg+Pythia8 but
807 pthard=1.
808 Parton shower modeling Difference between Powheg+Pythia8 and Powheg+Herwig7 samples.

822 9.2.3 𝒁 → 𝝂𝝂+fake tau
823 The (𝑍 → 𝜈𝜈) plus jet-faking tau is a minor source of the SM background in the SRs. As discussed in
824 Appendix D, to take account for the observed mismodeling of fake-tau rate in MC, we assign conservative
825 100% uncertainty on its normalization.
826 9.2.4 Other components
827 All the other background (diboson, 𝑍 → 𝜏𝜏+jets, etc.) are estimated by MC simulation directly, with
828 conservative normalization uncertainty of 30%.
829 9.2.5 Signal uncertainties
830 The signal uncertainties are evaluated by QCD scale variation and PDF uncertainties, followed by PMG
831 recommendation. Note that we use NNPDF 3.0 NLO (260800) as the baseline PDF choice. Therefore, we
do not have 𝛼𝑆 variations for this PDF set. As a result, 10%20% uncertainty based on different parameter ˜ 832
833 choices is assigned to the signals.

834 9.2.6 Summary of theoretical uncertainties considered
835 The estimated uncertainties for background samples are summarized in Table 9.1 and Table 9.2.
836 Some uncertainties estimated by the alternative MC samples are affected by the statistical fluctuation in the
837 tail region. For these uncertainties, smoothing of distributions is applied from the bulk region, to estimate
838 the tail distribution reasonably.
839 First, the alternative sample is normalized to the nominal sample in the dedicated CR. Then, the 𝑚𝑏𝜏
(resonance) or 𝐸
miss
T
840 (non-resonance) 𝑁 − 1 distribution is compared between them. The "353QH twice"
841 algorithm [48] implemented in ROOT is used to smooth the ratio. Finally, the smoothed ratio is applied to
the nominal distribution, and the yields in SR/VR at the higher 𝑚𝑏𝜏 or 𝐸
miss
T
842 are obtained for the given
843 systematic uncertainty, which corresponds to (1±Δ𝑇 𝐹/𝑇 𝐹). This procedure is performed for the following
844 systematic sources and regions.
845 • Resonance regions:
846 – Top PS in SR.
847 – Top ME in TopVR.
848 – Top FSR (down) in SR (relaxed, not smoothed).
849 – Top Hdamp in SR (relaxed, not smoothed)
850 – 𝑡𝑡¯ Recoil in SR (relaxed, not smoothed).
851 – Wt interference in TopVR.
852 • Non-resonance regions:
853 – W+jets QCD Scale (relaxed, not smoothed)
854 – Top PS in SR (relaxed, not smoothed).
855 – Top ME in SR (relaxed).
856 – Top Hdamp in SR (relaxed).
857 – 𝑡𝑡¯ Recoil in SR (relaxed).
858 – Wt interference in SR (relaxed, not smoothed).
859 For some uncertainties (marked as “relaxed”), MC statistical uncertainty is too large even with the
860 smoothing. For them, some selection cuts are relaxed to increase the statistics. We removed the requirement
861 of the number of jets and require 𝑁𝑏 ≥ 1 instead of 𝑁𝑏 = 1. If the evaluated uncertainty with the looser
862 selection cuts is reasonable, smoothing is not applied (marked as “relaxed, not smoothed”). Otherwise,
863 both smoothing and relax SR are used (“relaxed”).
Figures 9.1 and 9.2 show the 𝑚𝑏𝜏 and 𝐸
miss
T
864 distributions in resonance and non-resonance regions,
865 respectively, before and after the smoothing applied.
866 The validity of the procedures above are checked in Appendix H.

867 10 Statistical Analysis
868 10.1 TRexFitter Statistical Framework
869 The TRexFitter framework is used to perform the statistical analysis.
870 For the background-only fit, a maximum-likelihood fit is performed on the 4 CRs to determine the
871 normalization factors of 𝑊+jets and top-quark events (𝑡𝑡¯ plus single top), each of which has a single bin.
872 The post-fit normalization factors and nuisance parameters are applied to SRs and VRs to compare the data
873 with the SM expectations.
874 For the S+B fit, a maximum-likelihood fit is performed on the 4 CR + 2 SR event categories to determine
875 the signal and background normalization factors simultaneously. The signal acceptance in each category is
876 predicted by the simulation.
877 The likelihood function L (𝜇, 𝜆, ® 𝜃®) depends on:
878 • 𝜇: the signal strength,
• 𝜆® = {𝜆
Res
𝑊 , 𝜆Non-Res
𝑊 , 𝜆Res
𝑡
, 𝜆Non-Res
𝑡
879 }: the background normalization factors for 𝑊+jets and top-quark
880 samples, separated for Resonance and Non-Resonance regions, and
881 • 𝜃®: a set of nuisance parameters to take account for systematic uncertainties in the fit.
882 The correlation between BSM and Interference terms of the signal sample is considered by normalizing the
Interference sample by the factor √
883 𝜇. The number of signal and background yields in each SR and CR is
884 described by:
𝑁𝑒𝑥 𝑝 = 𝜇𝑁𝑠𝑖𝑔 (𝜃) + √
𝜇𝑁𝑖𝑛𝑡(𝜃) + 𝜆𝑊 𝑁
𝑀𝐶
𝑊 (𝜃) + 𝜆𝑡𝑁
𝑀𝐶
𝑡
(𝜃) + 𝑁
𝑀𝐶
𝑜𝑡 ℎ𝑒𝑟 𝑠 (𝜃), (10.1)
where the appropriate normalization factor (𝜆
Res or 𝜆
Non-Res 885 ) is applied depending on the category. The
886 likelihood is defined as:
L =
Ö
𝑆𝑅,𝐶𝑅
Pois(𝑁𝑜𝑏𝑠 |𝑁𝑒𝑥 𝑝) ×Ö
Gaus(𝜃|0, 1). (10.2)
887 A very limited effect on the exclusion limit is expected according to the study in Appendix I. For the
888 background-only fit, the signal strength 𝜇 is fixed to 0.
889 Both 𝜇 and 𝜆® are treated as free parameters in the fit. The 𝜃® allows variations of the expectations for signal
890 and background according to the systematic uncertainties, subject to Gaussian, log-normal or Poisson
891 constraints in the likelihood fit. Their fitted values represent the deviations from the nominal expectations
892 that globally provide the best fit to the data. Statistical uncertainties in each bin due to the limited size of the
893 simulated background samples are taken into account by dedicated parameters using the Beeston-Barlow
894 “lite” (one parameter per bin) technique [49].
73

ATLAS DRAFT
895 The test statistic is evaluated with the RooFit package [50]:
𝑞𝜇 = −2 ln
L (𝜇, 𝜆®
𝜇, 𝜃®
𝜇)
L (𝜇,ˆ
ˆ
𝜆, ®
ˆ
𝜃®)
, (10.3)
where 𝜇ˆ,
ˆ
𝜆® and ˆ
𝜃® are the best-fit 𝜇, 𝜆® and 𝜃® to maximize the likelihood, and 𝜆®
𝜇 and 𝜃® 896 𝜇 are the best-fit 𝜆® and
897 𝜃® with the fixed signal strength 𝜇.
898 The uncertainty on 𝜇ˆ, Δ𝜇ˆ, is evaluated by scanning over 𝜇 to find the range corresponding to 𝑞𝜇 < 1. An
899 estimation of the contribution from systematic uncertainties is found by subtracting in quadrature from Δ𝜇ˆ
900 the statistical uncertainty, which is determined by redoing the fit to data after fixing all NPs to their best-fit
901 values. The impact of each nuisance parameter is evaluated by repeating the fit with the parameter fixed to
902 its post-fit value ±1𝜎, while allowing all other parameters to float. The resulting shift in 𝜇ˆ with respect to
903 the nominal fit is taken as the impact of that nuisance parameter.
904 To test the null hypothesis, the test statistics 𝑞0 at 𝜇 = 0 is particularly important. If no signals exceeding the
905 SM background are observed, the upper limit on the signal strength 𝜇 is derived using the CLs method [51]
906 at each parameter point of the LQ model.
907 Both shape and normalization systematics pruning can be performed during the fit. The "shape" pruning
908 firstly normalizes the alternative distribution to the same yield as the nominal distribution. If the alternative
909 distribution differs from the nominal distribution by less than the threshold in each bin, the "shape"
910 component will be pruned. Since we are using 1-bin estimation for each region, all NPs are shape-dropped.
911 The normalization systematic pruning requires that the non-prunable systematics be above the threshold.
912 Currently, we set the threshold to 1% to simplify the fit model.
913 10.2 Background-only fit results
914 The background-only fit to the CRs with all systematic uncertainties currently available has been performed.
915 The fit result of the normalization factors shown in Figure 10.1 is then extrapolated to the SRs and VRs.
916 Notice that the normalization factors for Top backgrounds are in good agreement, whereas those for W+jets
917 are incompatible. This is considered due to the slope observed in the jet 𝑝T distribution in Section 8.1.
918 The summary of observed data in each VR compared with the pre-fit and the post-fit expectation are shown
919 in Figure 10.2 and Table 10.1. Except for the non-resonance VR0tau, all VRs show good agreement within
920 1𝜎. A detailed investigation for this specific region is performed in Appendix G. The difference of data
921 from the expectation divided by total statistical and systematic uncertainties is 2.3𝜎, and it seems to be a
922 statistical fluctuation.
923 Additional study to check the 0𝑏 → 1𝑏 extrapolation with the mass or 𝑚T reverted 1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 VRs is also
924 performed in Appendix M. As a result, we observed good agreement between data and MC prediction in
925 this region, which indicates that the extrapolation is reasonable.
926 The pull plots for nuisance parameters are summarized in Figure 10.3. No significant constraint is observed.
927 The correlation plots are shown in Figure 10.4. Notice that the correlation matrix threshold is set to 20%
here. The correlation between normalization factors for Top background 𝜆
𝑅𝑒𝑠
𝑡
and 𝜆
𝑁 𝑜𝑛−𝑅𝑒𝑠
𝑡
928 is coming
929 from the strong correlation with other NPs, for example, Tau energy scales. The sys/nom plots of highly
930 correlated NPs are also shown in Figure 10.5 and Figure 10.6. The impact of TES on top background
931 makes it highly correlated to the top normalization factors. As well as for the high-𝑝T muons for W+jets.

941 The sensitivity is dominated by the data statistical uncertainty. Figures 10.7, 10.8, 10.9 and 10.10 show the
942 ranking plot of the systematic uncertainties. As expected, since we are using the CRs to replace 𝜏 to 𝑒/𝜇,
943 and 𝑏-veto in the WCRs instead of 𝑏-jet, uncertainties related to tau and flavor tagging are not canceled
944 out and relatively large. In addition, the ad-hoc 30% theoretical uncertainty on the ratio of 𝑊+heavy
945 flavor to 𝑊+light flavor cross sections described in Section 9.2.1 is one of the dominant uncertainties. The
946 top-ranked MC stat uncertainty comes from the larger stat uncertainties of the signal samples we used
947 for the fit. Still, the impact of the stat uncertainty on the results is negligibly small since the analysis is
948 stats-limited.

957 11 Results
958 After the LPX subgroup approval, we are allowed to see the SR data. The unblinded results are shown
959 in this chapter. The signals used for the results are already updated with the final version of the signal
960 samples, which use the default weight instead of additional internal weights.
961 11.1 B-only fit
962 The background-only fit result discussed in Section 10.2 is applied to the SR. The results are shown in
963 Figure 11.1. The results are very similar to the background-only fit results in the CR shown in Figure 10.1,
964 indicating that the background estimation worked as expected. As summarized in the table 10.1, the
965 expected post-fit yields from the b-only fit are 2.69±0.71 and 1.88±0.65 for the resonance and non-resonance
966 SRs, respectively. We observed 3 and 2 events in the resonance and the non-resonance SRs, which agree
967 very well with the SM expectations. The pull plots are shown in Figure 11.2. No obvious constraint
968 observed. The correlation matrix is also shown in Figure 11.3. The summary plot with the data points in
969 the SRs is shown in Figure 11.4. Unblinded SR distribution is shown in Figure 11.5

999 11.4 Toy limits
1000 The toy limits are also performed due to the low statistics in the SRs. The number of toys is 10k, and the
1001 scan steps are 15 for each parameter point of the signals. The scan range of 𝜇 is optimized, where the
1002 minimum 𝜇 is 0, and the maximum 𝜇 is decided based on the +2𝜎 expectation of the asymptotic limit. The
1003 results compared with asymptotic limits are shown in Figures 11.14 and 11.15. The asymptotic expectation
1004 agrees well with the toy limits.

1005 12 Post ATLAS circulation updates
1006 During the ATLAS circulation, a large contamination was pointed out in the current WVRs from the large
𝛽
23
𝐿
signals. It was overlooked since we were looking at the low 𝛽
23
𝐿
1007 scenarios when we checked the signal
1008 contamination in the VRs, due to the delay of the signal production. There are no significant excess is
1009 observed in the WVRs. At the PAM, we agreed with the PC and conveners to add the WVRs in the fit as
1010 0b SRs. The additional studies regarding that are described in this section. Hereafter, WVR is denoted as
1011 SR0b.

1024 12.2 Interference study and signal model in 0b SR
1025 In SR0b, the interference effect with the SM 𝜏𝜈 processes is larger than SRs with a 𝑏-jet, because in the SM
1026 the production of a 𝜏𝜈 system with an associated 𝑏 quark is CKM-suppressed making the SM amplitude
1027 that interferes with new physics contribution highly reduced at the high-mass region. Since there are
1028 non-negligible impact from the interference to SR0bs, the fractions of the interference contribution to the
1029 pure BSM yields are computed at each point of the coupling parameter scan and summarized in Figure 12.6.
1030 Its impact is up to 20% at 𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV and 30~40% at higher LQ masses. It is confirmed that the
1031 interference effect does not cause the negative signal yields at the range of the coupling constants sensitive
1032 to this analysis. The interference effect is taken into account in the fit, by normalizing the interference term
by √
1033 𝜇 as discussed in Section. 10.1.
1034 12.3 Results with updated 0b + 1b SRs
1035 Since we do not change the selection cuts to define the SR0b, and the systematics uncertainties are already
1036 estimated for them, we simply include the SR0b to the likelihood of the fit. The B-only fit results, using
1037 CRs only in the likelihood, are the same as before.

Table 12.1 summarises the possible 𝜏𝜏 signal contamination in our SRs at 𝛽
23
𝐿
1062 = 0 and yields scaled to the
corresponding 𝛽
23
𝐿
1063 . We observed that the overall 𝜏𝜏 contamination can be around 35% in SR1b, and ~5% in
1064 SR0b. A possible 35% under-estimation of the signal yields is a bit larger than the theoretical uncertainties
1065 we currently consider (about 20%). We further estimated the possible impact on the sensitivity from
1066 the 𝜏𝜏 contamination. We scaled the nominal signal yields in SRs by additional constant normalisation
1067 factors, and reperformed the exclusion fit. The normalisation factors refer to the ratio of 𝜏𝜏 and 𝜏𝜈 yields
1068 in Table 12.1. Specifically, we applied factors of 1.37, 1.33, 1.05 and 1.07 to the signal yields in SR1b-Res,
1069 SR1b-NonRes, SR0b-Res and SR0b-NonRes, respectively.
1070 As a results, the limit on the signal strength 𝜇 is improved by 8%~14% depending on the 𝑀LQ, but the signal
strength is scaled to 𝑔
4
𝑈
and 𝛽
232
𝐿
1071 , so the improvement on the coupling constants is not so significant. We
1072 recalculated the 𝑔𝑈 limit and the results are summarised in the Tables 12.2 and 12.3. Possible improvements
1073 are about 5% at 1.8 TeV and about 2% at 3 TeV. Therefore, we would like to conclude that the missing 𝜏𝜏
1074 contribution gives the under-estimate of our sensitivity, but mostly covered by the theoretical uncertainty
1075 we are currently considering, and the possible improvement on the coupling constant is 2–5% depending
1076 on the mass.

1077 12.5 Interpretation with right-handed LQ signals
1078 Currently, we only have interference samples for 1.5 TeV right-handed signals. It was fine at the previous
1079 round of the analysis using SR1b only, since the interference effect is marginal, but as discussed above, it is
1080 not negligible in SR0b.
1081 We have several options for this part, to be decided.
1082 1. keep the visible cross-section limit discussion in the paper without considering interference.
1083 2. study the interference effect at 1.5 TeV and apply it to the other masses
1084 3. or, just drop the discussion of right-handed interpretation in the paper, as well as Figure 6 and Table
1085 8 in the auxiliary material.
1086 For the 2nd option, at 1.5 TeV, the interference effects are compared between pure left-handed and
1087 right-handed scenarios. The ratio of interference is summarized in the Table 12.4. We found that the
fraction is very close for |𝛽
33
𝑅
1088 | = 0 and 1 in resonance SR and in non-resonance SR with the coefficient
1089 2.65. Further study needs interference samples with other coupling parameters.

1093 13 Conclusion
1094 A first search for high-mass leptoquark signals with ATLAS is presented, targeting final states with a
1095 hadronically-decaying 𝜏 lepton, a 𝑏-jet and missing transverse momentum. Two signal regions are defined:
1096 one for the “resonance” signals, corresponding to a single production of the leptoquark decaying to 𝑏𝜏 in
1097 association with a neutrino, and the other for the “non-resonance” ones, considering the 𝑡-channel exchange
of the leptoquark, which manifests as an excess in the high-𝑚T tail of 𝜏 and 𝐸
miss
T
1098 . After the high-𝑝T 𝜏
and 𝐸
miss
T
1099 requirements, the fake tau contribution is strongly suppressed, and the main source of the SM
1100 background is (𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈)+jets and top-quark events containing real 𝜏 leptons. Dedicated control regions
1101 are defined for these backgrounds, and included in a profile-likelihood fit to give a constraint on their
1102 normalizations.
1103 In resonance and non-resonance SRs, 3 and 2 events are observed with the background expectations
1104 2.69 ± 0.71 and 1.88 ± 0.65. Given that no significant excess is observed in both SRs, the upper limits
on the 𝑈1 vector leptoquark couplings are set at 95% CL. For the pure left-handed scenario with 𝛽
33
𝑅
1105 = 0,
the observed (expected) upper limits on 𝑔𝑈 are 0.71(0.68) at 𝛽
23
𝐿
= 2.0 and 2.84(2.81) at 𝛽
23
𝐿
1106 = 0.2 for
leptoquark mass of 1.5 TeV, and 1.42(1.40) at 𝛽
23
𝐿
= 2.0 and 4.83(4.78) at 𝛽
23
𝐿
1107 = 0.2 for leptoquark mass
of 2.5 TeV. The results tested part of the region compatible with the 𝑅(𝐷
(∗)) anomaly at 𝛽
23
𝐿
1108 > 1.8.

1262 A Signal Validations
1263 A.1 Test samples for the initial study
1264 Two mass points of 1.5 TeV and 2.5 TeV were generated for the initial studies, using configurations
1265 e8531 (rel 23.6.10), and DAODs are listed in Table A.2. The 1.5 TeV samples were processed with both
1266 Geant4 (s3767) and AF3 (a907) but the 2.5 TeV was only with AF3. In the event generation, parameters of
the matrices in Eq. 4.1 were set to 𝑔𝑈 = 1 and 𝛽
23
𝐿
= 0.2. The 𝛽
33 1267 𝑅 was set to 0. The setting parameters are
1268 summarized in Table A.1. The tau is specified to be only hadronically decayed to enhance the signal events.
1269 The BSM events were further separated by BFilter/BVeto filters which checked the 𝑏-quark existence after
1270 Pythia: when 𝑏-quarks with |𝜂| <5 (any 𝑝T) are found, events are filtered/rejected, respectively. For the
1271 interference events, the sum of neutrino’s momenta in the ME (MadGraph), which is called net, was used
since we found a destructive effect of the interference term was mitigated at high-𝐸
miss
T
1272 region. We used
1273 net> 150 GeV/< 150 GeV, which corresponded to misset and missetmax in the MadGraph. Note that
1274 these variables don’t include the momenta of neutrinos from tau-decay, which is performed in the Pythia step.
1275 Only at 𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV, the additional “inclusive” samples not separated into BSM and Interference were
1276 generated for the validation. 10M events were generated for each sample. There are internal event weights
to vary 𝑔𝑈, 𝛽
23
𝐿
and 𝑀LQ parameters, so that we can scan them in the interpretation. The ranges of 𝑔𝑈, 𝛽
23
𝐿
1277 ,
1278 and 𝑀LQ are {0.5, 1, 1.5, 2.5, 3.5}, {0, 0.2, 0.6, 1, 1.4}, and {1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, 4, 5} (TeV), respectively. The
1279 other couplings were set to 0 since they are irrelevant to our signal (the 𝜅 parameter is also set to 0, so for
now we can test 𝑈
Yang-Mills
1280 1 model only).
1281 Figure A.1 shows some validation plots for the BSM part of the signals. The “Baseline” cuts for SRs
in Table 6.4 are applied, using the truth tau, truth lepton, truth jets and truth 𝐸
miss
T
1282 . The following set
of parameters is used: 𝑔𝑈 = 1.5/2.5, 𝛽
23
𝐿
= 1, 𝛽
33
𝑅
1283 = 0 and 𝜅 = 0. Here, samples are separated into
1284 “resonance” and “non-resonance” components by checking the parent particle’s PDG ID of 𝜏 in the final
1285 state. If it is the LQ, the event is categorized into the resonance part, otherwise non-resonance. For the
1286 resonance signals, the edge of the peak is found at 𝑚𝑏𝜏 = 1.5 TeV and 2.5 TeV, which corresponds to
1287 the LQ mass, due to the neutrinos in the tau lepton decay. On the other hand, the 𝑏𝜏 mass is decreased
1288 exponentially for the non-resonance part, not depending on the LQ mass.
1289 Figure A.2 shows some validation plots for the Interference part of the signals at 𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV. Here,
1290 the signal events are separated into the 𝑏-veto and 𝑏-tagged regions. The interference effect is small for the
1291 resonance part of the signals (consistent with zero). For the non-resonance signals, if we do not require any
1292 𝑏-jets in the events, a strong destructive interference effect is observed at 𝑚T < 800 GeV region, due to the
1293 large contribution of the SM 𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈. After the requirement of 𝑏-jets in the event, the destructive effect
1294 of the interference is mitigated, thanks to the small CKM matrix elements 𝑉𝑏𝑐 and 𝑉𝑏𝑢 and suppressed
𝑊+jets events, and it is found only at 𝑚T < 400 GeV. In the SRs with high-𝑝T 𝜏 (> 200 GeV), high 𝐸
miss
T
1295
1296 (> 400 GeV) and high 𝑚T (> 600 GeV), the interference effect is marginal.

1297 About 30% of the signals have a 𝑐-jet in the event. For the 𝑐-jet production via the LQ model assumed, the
1298 initial state must be a 𝑏 quark, so its contribution is relatively small. It is not included in the scope of this
1299 analysis, and we only focus on the 𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state.
1300 There were several issues in the test samples:
1301 • We have the internal weights to vary the leptoquark mass. It works fine for the non-resonant
1302 component of the signals, but doesn’t work well for the resonance part due to the poor statistics at the
1303 tail (it’s quite difficult to reproduce e.g. 2 TeV resonance by reweighting 1.5 TeV sample). For the
1304 final interpretation, we need to generate samples with each mass point at 1.5 TeV, 2 TeV, 2.5 TeV,
1305 3 TeV, 4 TeV and 5 TeV.
1306 • Events rejected by the BFilter/BVeto filters after Pythia are necessary to use the internal weights
1307 since MadGraph calculates the internal weights using all the produced events. Any events produced
1308 by MadGraph should be kept in order to use the internal weights. So, we have to remove the
1309 BFilter/BVeto filters.
• We would reweight the nominal sample with 𝛽
33
𝑅
= 0 to |𝛽
33
𝑅
| = 1
1
1310 , but it might not work well.
Therefore, for the final interpretations, we need at least two sets of samples for 𝛽
33
𝑅
1311 = 0 and −1 at each mass
1312 point of {1.5, 1.8, 2, 2.5, 3} TeV. On the other hand, if we need to interpret our results with the minimal
model, we need samples with 𝜅 = 0 having the full scan of 𝑔𝑈 and 𝛽
23
𝐿
1313 .
1 The choice of sign for 𝛽
33
𝑅
is important for low-energy observables, like 𝑅(𝐷
(∗)) anomalies, but not for the signatures at the
LHC. The reason for this is that the new physics amplitudes of different chiralities do not interfere with each other and the
amplitude proportional to 𝛽
33
𝐿
𝛽
33
𝑅
does not interfere with the SM ones [37]. Therefore we generate the samples with 𝛽
33
𝑅
= +1,
but it can be used for 𝛽
33
𝑅
= −1 which is preferred by low-energy observables, and collectively described as |𝛽
33
𝑅
| = 1.

For the limit setting, we use a single sample generated with 𝑔𝑈 = 1 and 𝛽
23
𝐿
1317 = 0.2 at each mass point,
1318 and vary these parameters using the MadGraph internal event weights. To validate the reliability of the
1319 weights, we also prepared a set of independent “reference” samples generated with the varied parameters.
1320 Cross sections and selected kinematic distributions are compared between the weighted and the reference
1321 samples.
1322 The ratio of cross sections for the weighted samples to those for the reference samples is shown in Figure A.3.
The definition of indices is summarized in Table A.3. The “baseline” parameters 𝑔𝑈 = 1.0 and 𝛽
23
𝐿
1323 = 0.2
1324 correspond to the index 9.
1325 Overall, the weighting method reproduces the reference well. The difference between the weighted and
1326 reference samples is typically less than 10%, particularly in the range 1.0 < 𝑔𝑈 < 4.0. At larger couplings
(𝑔𝑈 > 2.0), the method does not work for 𝛽
23
𝐿
1327 = 0.0, and there is a systematic trend of overestimating
the cross section at higher 𝛽
23
𝐿
1328 values, which becomes more pronounced as 𝑔𝑈 increases. At the lowest
1329 coupling (𝑔𝑈 = 0.5), a different trend is observed: the cross sections obtained from the weighting method
are up to a factor of two larger than those from the ref samples at 𝛽
23
𝐿
1330 < 0.2.
1331 At the parameter points where discrepancies in the cross sections are observed, differences are also found
1332 in several kinematic distributions. In these cases, the reweighting with the internal event weights alone
1333 cannot adequately absorb the differences between the resonance and non-resonance components. This
1334 also suggests that a one-dimensional reweighting using only the jet 𝑝T or the 𝑚𝑏𝜏 is insufficient, since
1335 it is difficult to simultaneously correct both components across different mass points. To address this,
1336 we propose an additional reweighting at the truth level using the two-dimensional distribution of the
1337 leading-jet 𝑝T and the 𝑚𝑏𝜏. First, the ratio of cross sections between the weighted and reference samples
1338 is applied as a global scale factor to adjust the overall normalization. Then, the ratio of weighted to
1339 reference samples is computed bin-by-bin in the two-dimensional histogram, and this is used to correct
1340 the shape. With these two variables, the resonance and non-resonance components can be effectively
1341 adjusted in a pseudo-independent manner. All the necessary variables are included in DAOD_PHYS.
1342 With this additional reweighting, we checked the number of signal events at each step of the cut flow,
1343 mimicking the SR selections with truth-level observables. We found that, at every step, the difference
1344 between the weighted and reference samples is less than 10% for both resonance and non-resonance SRs,
1345 which is smaller than the signal theoretical uncertainties. An example of the additional weights is shown in
1346 Figure A.4.
1347 The expected limits with and without the additional correction above are compared in Figure A.5. The
1348 sensitivity is significantly decreased when 𝑔𝑈 becomes large due to the over-estimation of the resonance
1349 signals.

miss
T
. The requirement of high 𝐸
miss
T
1362 can reject most of the multi-jet events,

carefully checked, and as shown in the figure C.1, we found that the 𝐸
miss
T
1364 in the remaining events comes
from a mis-measurement of the high-energy hadronic jet. These events have a small Δ𝜙 between 𝐸
miss

1365 and
a jet. Meanwhile, the real 𝐸

1387 D Validation of 𝒁+fake-tau contribution
A possible source of fake 𝜏 contributions in the SRs, which require high 𝐸
miss
T
1388 , is 𝑍 → 𝜈𝜈+jets. Its
1389 contribution in each SR is 10% or less, so it is estimated by the MC simulation directly with conservative
1390 uncertainties. Since the modeling of the fake 𝜏 rate is not reliable in the simulation, the ZVR is defined
1391 to study it. The ZVR requires two “signal” muons/electrons and exactly one hadronic tau. The dilepton
1392 mass is required to be consistent with the 𝑍-boson mass. To ensure the data statistics, a 𝑏-veto and looser
1393 selection cuts than the SRs are applied, as summarized in Table D.1. Both electron and muon channels are
1394 utilized to further increase the data statistics. The 𝑍-boson 𝑝T is, as with the SR, reconstructed by jet and
tau 𝑝T, i.e. | ®𝑝
𝑙𝑙
T
+ ®𝑝
𝑚𝑖𝑠𝑠
T
|, to subtract the muon/electron contribution from the 𝐸
miss
T
1395 . On top of that, a loose
cut on 𝑝
𝑙𝑙
T
1396 is applied to suppress 𝑡𝑡¯ background and increase the purity of 𝑍+jets.

1397 The 116 data events are observed in ZVR. The tau 𝑝T and the RNN score distributions are shown in

1404 contribution to the SRs is minor. Therefore, we adopt the MC-based estimation for 𝑍+jets and assign an
1405 additional 100% uncertainty to account for the observed mismodeling.

1406 E Sensitivity projection to Run 3 and HL-LHC
As discussed in Section 3, the signal strength scales as 𝑔
4
𝑈
𝛽
2
23 1407 . Therefore, the sensitivity to the couplings is
1408 expected to improve only slowly with increasing integrated luminosity.
The expected limits on 𝛽
23
𝐿
and 𝑔𝑈 for different integrated luminosities of 140 fb−1
(Run-2 only), 450 fb−1
1409
(Run-2 + Run-3) and 3000 fb−1
1410 (HL-LHC) are shown in Figure E.1. The benchmark mass points at
1411 𝑀LQ = 2 TeV and 3 TeV are shown. The center-of-mass energy is fixed at 13 TeV. And Asimov data is used
1412 in all regions. The selection cuts are not reoptimized for the higher luminosity scenario. The improvement
on the limits by adding Run-3 data is about 1𝜎. Even with 3000 fb−1
1413 , it is about 2𝜎 level. Note that here
1414 we interpolate the 𝜇 = 1 line in between the grids to draw the limit with ROOT function, whereas the signal
1415 strength 𝜇 is proportional to the fourth power of the coupling 𝑔𝑈. Therefore the actual difference of the
1416 limits between Run-2, Run-2+Run-3 and HL-LHC should be a bit smaller than 1 or 2 𝜎. Of course we can
1417 further optimize the selections, and a small gain is expected by increasing the center-of-mass energy to
1418 13.6 or 14 TeV, but with this approach, a careful step-by-step verification of the 𝐵-anomaly favored region
1419 will be necessary over the next 10-20 years.

1420 F Control region study for e/𝝁 difference
1421 This appendix introduces a detailed study of the control region used to determine the normalization of
1422 𝑊+jets (WCRs). As the results shown in the Section 8, we observed the discrepancy between e- and
1423 mu-channels in the data/MC ratios. Especially for Non-Resonance WCR, the data/MC ratio is 1.00 ± 0.09
1424 for the electron channel, while 0.80±0.08 for the muon channel, corresponding to the 1.7 𝜎 discrepancy.
1425 To investigate this, we first carefully checked the all relevant MC corrections, separately applied in
1426 the e and mu channels, e.g. trigger efficiency SF, and confirmed they are correctly applied. We then
1427 checked the data/MC ratio at each step of the cut flow. The discrepancy appears after applying the cut
50 < 𝑝 𝑗𝑒𝑡
T
1428 < 250 GeV requirement for Non-Resonance WCR. The data-to-data and MC-to-MC comparisons
1429 on jet 𝑝T distribution between e- and mu-channels are performed, and shown in Figure F.1. The e- and
1430 mu-channels are generally consistent in both data and MC. The e-channel has slightly higher yields in the
1431 data and slightly less in the MC, but within the statistical uncertainty.
1432 An upward fluctuation is observed in the electron channel in data around 80–100 GeV. For MC, we checked
1433 the event weight distribution, including cross-section, efficiency SFs, pileup weight etc., for electron and
1434 muon channels, as a function of jet 𝑝T. As shown in Figure F.2, some events have abnormally large weight,
around 30 for a single event. These large weighted events are mainly distributed in 𝑝
𝑗𝑒𝑡
T
1435 < 50 GeV, but
1436 sometimes, (maybe) due to pileup overlay conditions, randomly found in higher 𝑝T region. In our WCR,
1437 they are not found in electron channel, but there is one event in muon channel at 𝑝T > 50 GeV. This makes
1438 the MC yields in the muon channel higher than the electron channel.
1439 In summary, the observed discrepancy arises from fluctuations in electron data and muon MC, both likely
1440 statistical in nature. To improve statistical precision, we will combine the electron and muon channels
1441 and use a common normalization factor in the final fit. To ensure the robustness of this approach, we will
1442 validate the modeling by carefully checking the post-fit data/MC agreement in the validation regions.
142

=== PDF PAGE 143 ===
ATLAS DRAFT
Figure F.1: Electron and muon channel comparison of leading jet 𝑝T in the Non-resonance WCR for data (left) and
MC (right). The red line shows the leading jet 𝑝𝑡 for electron channel and the blue line shows the one for muon
channel. 2.6 𝜎 and 0.7 𝜎 discrepancies are observed for data and MC samples, respectively.
Figure F.2: Event weight distribution as a function of leading jet 𝑝T in Non-resonance WCR for electron (left) and

1443 G Validation region study
1444 This section investigates the post-fit discrepancy between data and MC observed in the Non-Resonant
1445 VR0tau, as discussed in Section 10.2. The difference of observed data from the SM expectation is 2.84𝜎
1446 when including all systematic uncertainties currently available. It is reduced to about 2.5𝜎 if we include
1447 the additional 20% uncertainty on 𝑊+jets heavy flavor modeling uncertainty as discussed in Section 9.
1448 Furthermore, the discrepancy is reduced to about 2.3𝜎 if we include the theory uncertainties that cannot be
1449 canceled between SRs/VRs and CRs as we summarized in Table 9.1 and Table 9.2.
1450 The pre-fit data/MC ratio in each step of the cutflow (table 7.1) is shown in Figure G.1. The data/MC
1451 ratio remains close to unity after the jet multiplicity requirement. The discrepancy arises after applying
𝐸
miss
T
> 400 GeV. The 𝐸
miss
T
1452 N-1 distribution in VR0tau is shown in Figure G.2. The data generally agree
well with the MC, except for a specific 𝐸
miss
T
1453 range between 425 GeV and 450 GeV.
If we loosen the 𝐸
miss
T
1454 cut to 300 GeV, the data/MC discrepancy is relaxed to 1.8𝜎 (stat-only). Conversely,
if we tighten the 𝐸
miss
T
1455 cut to 500 GeV, the discrepancy is also improved to 1.5𝜎 (stat-only). This suggests
that the excess is localized within a narrow 𝐸
miss
T
1456 range.
Figure G.3 shows the lepton 𝑝T distribution in the range 425 < 𝐸miss
T
1457 < 450 GeV. The data excess is observed
1458 only in 300–400 GeV bin, so it is not coming from the threshold effect of the lepton 𝑝T cut (> 200 GeV).
We examined the 12 data events in this region (425 < 𝐸miss
T
< 450 GeV, 300 < 𝑝ℓ
T
1459 < 400 GeV), which
1460 consist of 7 in the electron channel and 5 in the muon channel. Figure G.4 shows the 𝜂–𝜙 map of the
1461 lepton and 𝑏-jet. These events are not clustered in any specific detector region, suggesting no localized
1462 detector-related issue.
1463 In a nutshell, the excess appears to be a statistical fluctuation. The probability of observing a 2.3𝜎
discrepancy in at least one out of six independent VRs is approximately 20% (1 − (96.4%)
6
1464 ).

1465 H Validation for theory uncertainties estimation.
1466 As described in Section 9, we estimated some theoretical systematic uncertainties for the background
1467 process with smoothing or loose selection cuts to mitigate the impact of the MC statistical uncertainty.
1468 This section discusses the validity of those methods to estimate the uncertainty reasonably.
1469 To estimate some uncertainties, we relaxed the requirements of jet and 𝑏-jet multiplicities. If the systematic
1470 uncertainty to be estimated is not correlated to the number of jets and 𝑏-jets, it is fine to use it to estimate
1471 the systematic uncertainty for the actual SRs/VRs. For TopISR uncertainty in the resonance SR, we have
1472 enough MC statistics and we do not need to relax any selection cuts. We tried to relax jet multiplicity
1473 requirement in that region, and re-estimate the uncertainty. The result is shown in Figure H.1. The resulting
1474 uncertainty 1 + Δ𝑇 𝐹/𝑇 𝐹 is changed from 0.96 to 0.92. The difference between the results for SR and
1475 relaxed SR is less than 5%, which is acceptable compared with the statistical errors of 0.29.
Figure H.1: Comparison of systematics and nomianal N-1 distribution of 𝑀𝜏+𝑏 in the Resonance SR (left) and 𝑁𝑗𝑒𝑡𝑠
relaxed Resoannce SR (right). The uncertainties for the two cases are -0.04 and -0.08, respectively.
1476 The validity of applying smoothing for the N-1 distribution is checked for the Top ME uncertainty in
1477 resonance TopVR. Some selection cuts are relaxed to increase the statistics: 𝑁𝑏 >= 2 instead of 𝑁𝑏 = 2;
1478 and the jet multiplicity requirement is removed. The N-1 distributions with and without smoothing are
1479 shown in Figure H.2. The estimated uncertainties are found to be 0.27 ± 0.10 and 0.15 ± 0.12 before and
1480 after the smoothing, respectively. The results are consistent between each other, which indicates that the
1481 smoothing is working as expected.

uncertainties are 0.27 ± 0.10 and 0.15 ± 0.12 before and after smoothing, respectively.

1482 I Study for the Signal interference with SM
1483 This section studies the interference contribution in the SRs. For now, the interference sample is available
only for 𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV. The interference contribution at different (𝛽
23
𝐿
1484 , 𝑔𝑈) sets is obtained using the
internal weights. The ratio of 𝑁𝑖𝑛𝑡/𝑁𝐵𝑆𝑀 in a 2-dimensional plane of 𝛽
23
𝐿
1485 –𝑔𝑈 is shown in figure I.1.
1486 The Resonance-only and Non-resonance-only limits are also presented in the plots. In the resonance
1487 SR, the interference contribution is typically < 5% at all parameter points. In the non-resonance SR,
1488 its contribution is up-to 40% at the lowest 𝑔𝑈 region, but the signal yields at that region are quite low
1489 and cannot be excluded by the current analysis. At the larger coupling, around the 95% CL limit by the
1490 non-resonance SR alone, the interference contribution is typically less than 10% at 𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV. At the
1491 low-mass region at 1.5 TeV, the sensitivity of the non-resonance SR is low compared to the resonance SR.
1492 The expected 95% CL limit compared with BSM-only fit is shown in Figure I.2. The interference is small
1493 enough to be ignored during the fit. At 3 TeV, where the non-resonance SR is dominant, the interference
1494 contribution is found to be less than 5 % around the limit.
1495 By fitting with the final signal samples, we observed little difference between the limits w/wo interference
1496 as shown in Figure I.2. Hence, we concluded that, in the region of large coupling where the analysis has
1497 sufficient sensitivity, the interference effect is small enough.

1498 J Impact of 𝑾+heavy flavor fraction uncertainty
1499 A normalization discrepancy of 10-30% between data and simulation by requiring heavy-flavor tags is
1500 reported by Physics Modeling Group (TWiki).
1501 Since we require a single 𝑏-jet in the SR and vetoing the 𝑏-jets in WCR, the discrepancy between data
1502 and simulation cannot be evaluated in the CR, which may lead to a maximum 30% uncertainty while
1503 extrapolating the 𝑊+jets normalization from CR to SR. Currently, we are taking the uncertainty as the
1504 medium value 20%. Variations of 10/20/30% are tested according to the instructions. The 95% CL
1505 limits while applying different uncertainties are shown in Figure J.1. The ranking plots are also shown in
1506 Figure J.2. The HF modeling uncertainty comes to the leading NP when we set it to 30% using signal
𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV, 𝑔𝑈 = 1.5, 𝛽23
𝐿
1507 = 0.6. Meanwhile, the expected significance values for the three variations
1508 are 2.156, 2.105, and 2.034, respectively. As a result, the increase in 𝑊+jets heavy-flavor modeling
1509 uncertainty leads to a maximum 5% decrease in significance.
1510 We also compared the fit configurations for HF uncertainties correlated and decorrelated between two
1511 SRs. The sensitivities are almost the same, but the ranking of uncertainties is changed. At the lower mass
1512 region, the uncertainty for the resonance SR gives higher impact to the sensitivity, while at the higher mass
1513 region, both resonance and non-resonance uncertainties are important. The ranking of decorrelated HF
1514 uncertainties is shown in Figure J.3. Given that the 𝑊+heavy flavor/light flavor ratio can depend on 𝑝T, we
1515 will use decorrelated NPs as a nominal configuration.
151

1520 L A choice of WCR
1521 The normalization factor for 𝑊+jets is estimated in 0𝜏0𝑏 WCR, and extrapolated to the 1𝜏1𝑏 SR. Following
1522 the PMG recommendation, as discussed in Appendix J, the additional uncertainty on 𝑊+heavy flavor/light
1523 flavor ratio is considered, but the pre-fit value 20% is a bit arbitral. We checked the validity in 0𝜏1𝑏 VRs,
1524 and observed 2𝜎 mild excess, as discussed in Appendix G. In this appendix, we tested the different choice
1525 of WCR.
1526 At the unblinding approval, the following 3 options were suggested by LPX conveners:
1527 1. Use the 0𝜏0𝑏 CR in the fit, and test the validity in 0𝜏1𝑏 VR (the current choice);
1528 2. Use the 0𝜏1𝑏 VR as the WCR, and omit the 𝑊+HF/LF uncertainty from the fit;
1529 3. Use both 0𝜏0𝑏 and 0𝜏1𝑏 regions as the WCRs in the fit, to give a constraint on 𝑊+HF/LF uncertainty.
1530 The S+B fit is performed and the corresponding limit is calculated for each option. The signal samples
used in this test is 𝑀LQ = 2.5 TeV, 𝑔𝑈 = 2.5, 𝛽23
𝐿
1531 = 1.0. The obtained normalization factors in the different
1532 fit configurations are shown in the Figure L.1. For the option 2, since the number of data events in 0𝜏1𝑏
1533 region is extremely smaller than the current 0𝜏0𝑏 WCR, the post-fit uncertainty on 𝜆𝑊 is 7–8 times larger
than the option 1. The post-fit pull for 𝜆
𝑁 𝑜𝑛𝑅𝑒𝑠 1534 𝑊 is 1.91 ± 0.52. The summary plots and NP ranking plots
1535 are shown in Figure L.2 and Figure L.3. For the option 3, about 1𝜎 pull is observed for the 𝑊+jets HF
1536 modleing uncertainty, as expected, and the post-fit uncertainty is constrained to about 0.9.

𝜇 = 1.0

1542 between each other. The 𝑊+jets yield in non-resonance SR is increased for the option 2 and 3, compared
1543 to the option 1, due to the larger 𝜆𝑊 and the pulled 𝑊+HF uncertainty, respectively. The limit plots for
1544 different options are also shown in Figure L.4.

1545 M 1𝝉0𝒍1𝒃 VR study
1546 This section discusses the additional study of the 1𝜏0ℓ0𝑏 VR. As introduced in the Section 7, SR is defined
1547 with the 1𝜏0ℓ1𝑏 selections. To define the VR with the same 1𝜏0ℓ1𝑏 selections, we need to use the different
1548 kinematic cuts from the SR, for example with the looser 𝑚𝑏𝜏 and/or 𝑚T, which leads to the additional
1549 systematic uncertainty only on these VRs. We therefore avoided to use such VRs, and used 0𝜏1ℓ1𝑏 region
1550 to validate the 0𝑏 → 1𝑏 extrapolation, where we found the discrepancy between data and simulation after
1551 performing the background-only fit, as discussed in Appendix G. Although it seems that the discrepancy
1552 mostly comes from the statistical fluctuation, it would be helpful to find an additional 1𝑏 VR to verify this
1553 conclusion. New VRs with the 1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 selections are defined by reverting the invariant mass and 𝑚T cut in
1554 resonance and non-resonance SR, respectively:
𝑚𝑏𝜏 > 800 GeV → 400 < 𝑚𝑏𝜏 < 800 GeV (M.1)
1555 for the resonance 1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 VR, and
𝑚T(𝜏, 𝐸miss
T
) > 600 GeV → 500 < 𝑚T(𝜏, 𝐸miss
T
) < 600 GeV (M.2)
1556 for the non-resonance 1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 VR. In this study, we do not consider any additional systematic uncertainties
1557 on the 𝑚𝑏𝜏 and 𝑚T modeling.
Additionally, the 𝑚T and 𝑝
𝜏
T
1558 requirements are loosened by 100 GeV in the resonant VR. For the non-resonant
VR, the 𝐸
miss
T
threshold is lowered by 200 GeV and the 𝑝
𝜏
T
1559 requirement is loosened by 100 GeV to increase
1560 the statistical yield.
1561 The signal contamination in these new 1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 VRs is checked before examining the data. For the coupling
1562 parameters, we choose the same ones as those used in Sec. 7.3. The signal contamination is 2% in the
1563 resonance VR and 7% in the non-resonance VR.
1564 The normalization factors derived by the background-only fits to CRs are applied to these new VR. The
1565 post-fit number of background yields is (25.4 ± 1.5) and (8.0 ± 1.7) for resonance and non-resonance VR,
1566 respectively, and the observed data are 24 and 9. The numbers of data events in the lower mass regions of
1567 SRs are well described by the current background estimation model.

1568 N SR0b optimisation
During the ATLAS circulation, we found that the contamination of signals with large 𝛽
23
𝐿
1569 in VRW-Res
1570 and VRW-NonRes could be significant. Therefore we adjust the fit strategy and use WVR-Res and
1571 WVR-NonRes as our new SR0b-Res and SR0b-NonRes in the fit since we can gain sensitivity from both
1572 regions. Meanwhile, the discussion of the necessity of the optimisation for the SR0b is raised naturally.
1573 Since we observed a large interference contribution in the current SR0b and many background events
1574 remaining, we expect less interference effect and less SM background yields with considerable signal
1575 yields after the cut optimisation, which finally leads to a better sensitivity.
1576 For simplicity, we performed the optimisation with only a few leading systematics for both SR0b-Res and
SR0b-NonRes. The signal parameter (𝑀LQ, 𝑔𝑈, 𝛽23
𝐿
1577 ) of (1.5 TeV, 0.5, 1.8) and (3.0 TeV, 2.0, 1.8) are used
for SR0b-Res and SR0b-NonRes optmisation, respectively. The 𝐸
miss
T
1578 cut is scanned from 200 GeV to
1579 1500 GeV with a scan step of 100 GeV to find the cut that maximizes the significance. The significance
1580 scan results are shown in the Figure N.1. The best points are 600 GeV and 400 GeV for SR0b-Res and
SR0b-NonRes, respectively. For SR0b-NonRes, the current definition is the same as the optimized 𝐸
miss
T
1581
1582 cut. So there is no need to change the SR0b-NonRes definition. For SR0b-Res, as shown in Figure N.2, we
1583 observed a maximum 30% improvement and 10% improvement at 1.5 TeV and 2.0 TeV, respectively. The
interference effect is also significantly suppressed due to the high 𝐸
miss
T
1584 cut as shown in Figure N.3. Since
1585 the Resonance process contribution is much less than the non-resonance process at higher mass, only 2%
1586 improvement is expected at 3.0 TeV.

miss
T
for SR0b-Res and SR1b-NonRes, respectively. Only a few leading
systematics are considered.
